With Search Traffic Down 70%, People Inc. Bets On Revenue Beyond Site Visits

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

People Inc. isn’t mincing words about the current state of digital publishing: It’s bad.

The company has seen a “critical loss of sessions,” Barry Diller, People Inc.’s chairman and senior executive, told investors during the company’s Q3 earnings call Tuesday morning.

People Inc.’s referral traffic from Google search is down 70% over the past two years, dropping 22% in Q3 after a 40%% drop in Q2.

AI Overview summaries now appear at the top of 90% of Google searches associated with People Inc.’s content, said CEO Neil Vogel.

Traffic declines aren’t just limited to Google search, though. People Inc.’s core sessions were down 26% in Q3, the biggest single-quarter decline the company has seen yet.

That traffic is gone, and it isn’t coming back, Vogel conceded to investors. The loss contributed to People Inc. laying off 195 employees, about 6% of its staff, last week.

However, People Inc. believes that it has weathered the worst of Google’s AI search overhaul and that the biggest referral traffic drops are behind it.

Vogel emphasized that, despite its traffic challenges, People Inc.’s digital revenue is still growing, albeit slowly.

Digital revenue rose 2% year over year in Q3, to $289 million, marking People Inc.’s twelfth consecutive quarter of digital growth, even as total Q3 revenue for its overall media business was down 5% to $409.9 million.

Despite revenue declines, Vogel credits People Inc.’s ongoing digital growth to the fact it was able to predict changes to the digital publishing model.

People Inc. coined the term “Google Zero” after all, he noted. Anticipating that most search traffic will disappear, People Inc. has spent the past three years transforming its business to focus more on non-session revenue and advertising products like its D/Cipher contextual targeting tech, he said.

The time is right for these investments to start paying off, and the company should see the first major returns in 2027, according to CFO Tim Quinn.

That better be the case. Diller said he would be “very disappointed” if People Inc. didn’t return to double-digit growth next year.

Looking ahead, People Inc. plans to focus on growing non-session revenue, meaning everything that isn’t tied to website visits. Non-session revenue already accounts for 43% of its digital revenue and grew 15% YOY in Q3.

By comparison, session-based revenue – which accounts for the remaining 57% – was down 6% last quarter.

People Inc.’s non-session growth priorities include live events and activations and content licensing deals with news aggregators, including Apple News and Yahoo, and AI companies, such as OpenAI, Meta and Microsoft.

Licensing revenue grew 49% in Q3, led by content deals with aggregator platforms and AI vendors. But this growth will slow a bit in Q4 as People Inc. laps the licensing deal it announced with Meta last year, Quinn said. That deal boosted last year’s Q4 numbers, which makes for a tougher comparison.

People Inc. has been exploring licensing its publishing brands for product and retail opportunities, but there’s been little sign of a return so far. For example, it’s been talking about releasing a Southern Living-branded sweet tea line for the better part of a year, but the product has yet to launch.

Also in the non-session revenue mix is D/Cipher, which People Inc. extended last year to include contextual targeting on third-party sites in addition to its own properties. D/Cipher’s targeting capabilities extend to CTV, which People Inc. sees as an untapped growth opportunity, Vogel said.

People Inc. is also expanding its non-session footprint on the digital media side, chiefly by launching more video series on social platforms.

One example is “The Intern,” a short-form video series featuring the InStyle editorial staff, which People Inc. is monetizing by allowing brands to sponsor a full season comprising seven three-minute episodes. “We weave that sponsor into the plot line in a wink, wink way that users enjoy,” Vogel said. “They then have the opportunity to layer on events at InStyle, events at People, media across YouTube and Instagram and across all these other places.”

While a show like “The Intern” is “very lucrative and very high margin” on its own, Vogel added, the opportunity to package other digital inventory and live activations into the sponsorship feels “very modern and very exciting for advertisers.”

People Inc. is also packaging more marketing opportunities across its highest-profile brands.

For example, Vogel pointed to People’s revamped “Sexiest Man Alive” offering, which this year features Noah Wyle, star of “The Pitt.”

“What started as a magazine cover, then a web feature, has become a robust franchise that lives across Instagram, TikTok, YouTube, Apple News, our People app and VIP subscription, podcasts and our distribution partners,” Vogel said. “We grew revenue associated with SMA this year 50%.”

The picture is less rosy elsewhere, though. Vogel did what he could to sugarcoat the 6% Q3 decline in session-based revenue by noting it could have been worse. After all, web sessions were down 26%.

But People Inc. offset the traffic decline with higher premium ad rates for both programmatic and direct-sold inventory, Quinn said.

Still, People Inc.’s advertising revenue was down 8% YOY in Q3. Its plan to get back to growth is to keep demonstrating strong performance for advertisers to compensate for traffic declines, Quinn said.

That being said, People Inc.’s performance marketing revenue growth slowed from 13% in Q2 to 2% in Q3. This slowdown was largely due to Amazon Prime Day falling in Q2, which shifted roughly 4% of growth to the earlier quarter, Quinn said.

But People Inc. remains bullish on the opportunity to reach readers directly through its owned-and-operated apps, including MyRecipes and the People VIP paid subscription tier. It’s exploring ways to monetize these apps further, Vogel said.

However, People Inc. has abandoned its plans to parlay its stake in MGM Resorts into a bid to take the company private, Diller said, though it still owns 26% of MGM’s stock and expects to buy more.

Diller also rejected a proposal by MGM to acquire People Inc., he said, adding that he doesn’t see any other proposals meeting his expectations for a sale. Meanwhile, pulling back from the MGM deal should free up some capital for People Inc. to reinvest in the company and buy back its own stock.

People Inc. is open to making its own acquisitions and plans to aggressively pursue M&A opportunities, Diller said, though he noted that any acquisitions would likely be small and “bolt-ons to the publishing business.”

In other news, Quinn said that People Inc. is still holding out hope for a nine-figure damages payout in its ad tech antitrust lawsuit against Google. He noted that USA Today’s Google lawsuit is moving forward and that People Inc.’s is next in line.

If it comes, a court-mandated payout may just be the last big check People Inc. gets from Google for the foreseeable future.

Correction 11/3/26: This article was updated to clarify the difference between People Inc.’s decline in Google search traffic and overall declines in its core sessions. We’ve also updated People Inc.’s overall revenue numbers to focus solely on the company’s media business, rather than revenue for the entire parent company.

There is a powerful undercurrent of disaffection and outright anger from the American populace toward US sporting institutions right now. But this isn’t just about logos cluttering the playing field. Sports leagues and teams are making decisions that sacrifice player safety and the enjoyment of their own fans in various bids for marketing revenue.

On Tuesday, Chord, a six-year-old commerce tech startup, acquired Zenlytic, an AI startup with an anthropomorphized data analyst agent named “Zoë,” for $5 million.

If you ask the executive leadership of Affle and Perion why the two companies are merging, the answer you’ll get is “synergies.”

A combo of Tubi, sports and political ads powered Fox’s growth this last quarter, even as Fox News lost viewers and the Roku deal awaits DOJ clearance.

It’s fall, and M&A is in the air. Two acquisitions happened this week: Stagwell bought Freestar, and DoorDash bought Instacart.

Starting in Q4, Reddit will test logged-in-only mode to protect its user engagement, improve ad targeting and keep AI from scraping its content.

Apple Watch’s always-listening AI tools arrive in the iOS 27.3 beta

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The update also brings back the redesigned Health app and Apple's first AI features for China before December.

Apple released the first developer betas of iOS 27.3 and watchOS 27.3. They add Audio Intelligence for the latest Apple Watches, a redesigned Health app pulled from iOS 27.2, and Apple Intelligence for China. The full release is due in December.

Apple has released the first developer betas of iOS 27.3 and watchOS 27.3, putting the Apple Watch’s always-listening AI tools into wider testing, Bloomberg reported on Tuesday. The full release is due in December.

The Audio Intelligence features, unveiled in September for the Series 12 and Ultra 4, use the watch’s microphone to follow nearby conversations. Siri Recap turns them into bullet points, while Live Rewind shows a transcript of the last 15 seconds.

The tools work in English only for now. Apple says the audio is processed on the device and not stored, and that the watch plays a sound when Live Rewind is used.

Lawyers in several US states have warned the features could break eavesdropping laws that require everyone in a conversation to agree to being recorded.

The beta also brings back Apple’s redesigned Health app, which it tested in iOS 27.2 but removed in October. It adds a Longevity tab and a Health Age score that estimates a user’s age from their health data, features that rivals such as Whoop and Oura already offer.

iOS 27.3 also carries Apple’s first AI features for China, including writing tools and Genmoji. They leave out Siri AI, which runs on Google models that are not available in China.

New Camping Gear We’re Testing (and Liking) This Fall

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Rainproof apparel, heated pads for the tent, and equipment for the fall camping season.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

HELLO, SHOULDER SEASON. Easily one of the best and (sometimes) overlooked times for camping, fall has all kinds of intrigue. From fewer people and colorful foliage to a crispness to the air and the possibility of high-elevation snow, there’s so much to be drawn to.

Sure, the temperature will fall, but that’s no reason to stow away the sleeping bag and tent for the year. Instead, add a few pieces of gear here and there to set yourself up for the best fall camping outing imaginable. That could be anything from a proper car camping mattress to another layer of apparel. Of course, a little waterproof gear and some added light-producers will only help your cause.

So, keep that camp stove within reach as we slip into November and December. There’s a decent chance your favorite campsite is still open and better still, not subject to a waiting list.

Ourselves, we’ve still been sleeping in the outdoors and trekking on trails—it’s a year-round pursuit of testing gear for us. With the change of the season, we wanted to highlight 15 new items we’re liking as we settle into the fall camping season, all tested in-house to make sure they’re worthy of your hard-earned cash and time.

As fall sets in, it’s good to fortify. That means protecting your belongings with another weatherproof layer. A waterproof soft cooler like this is a great way to do so as it functions more like a spacious messenger bag ready for any weather forecast. I’ve been taking mine out on the river to fly-fish or slinging it over my back for a bike commute, even in the rain. The shoulder strap is uber-comfortable, the extra webbing loops are great for attaching more gear (per a longer camping trip), and the insulation is impressive. How Video Gamers Make Split-Second Decisions

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

A cognitive psychology investigation from the University of Sheffield reveals that high-level video gamers process visual information and execute physical responses significantly faster than novices. Testing over 300 Counter-Strike players, researchers found that experts with over 5,000 hours of gameplay held an average 90-millisecond reaction advantage. Using Drift-Diffusion Modelling, the team showed that this speed boost stems from faster visual absorption, swifter motor execution, and more efficient evidence accumulation under pressure, though it confers no advantage in multitasking.

First-person shooter (FPS) games require players to navigate unpredictable environments, track multiple moving targets, and fire with sub-second accuracy. Among competitive titles, Counter-Strike stands as one of the most durable and influential tactical shooters in gaming history, sustaining a massive global esports ecosystem across nearly three decades.

Despite its worldwide popularity, neuroscientists and cognitive psychologists have rarely examined how thousands of hours of intense visual-motor competition reshape the mental architecture of its elite players.

In a study published in the American Psychological Association’s journal Experimental Psychology: Human Perception and Performance, cognitive scientists at the University of Sheffield conducted the first comprehensive examination of processing velocity in competitive Counter-Strike players, assessing how sustained gaming expertise translates into decision-making speed.

The research team, led by doctoral researcher Eleanor Hyde alongside senior author Professor Claudia von Bastian, recruited over 300 active players. Participants spanned the entire experience spectrum, from absolute novices to elite competitors logging in excess of 5,000 lifetime gameplay hours.

When exposed to standardized visual reaction tasks, the expert cohort demonstrated an average 90-millisecond advantage over novices. In rapid, real-world contexts, a 90-millisecond discrepancy represents a substantial operational margin, translating into meters of stopping distance during high-speed vehicle braking or deciding split-second tactical actions during critical emergencies.

“Although it may not sound like a lot, 90 milliseconds could be the difference between life and death in situations requiring rapid reactions, such as stopping quickly to avoid a road traffic collision,” explained Eleanor Hyde, lead author of the study at Sheffield’s School of Psychology.

“However, at this stage, we don’t have the data to determine whether this reaction speed is a result of playing the game for hours on end, or if people with naturally faster processing abilities gravitate towards these games. If it’s the former, it may be that training people in middle age or later life on such games could improve cognitive performance. If it’s the latter, reaction speed could serve as a useful indicator of skills crucial for high-stakes professions such as emergency response, air traffic control and driving.”

To pinpoint where the brain shaves off these critical milliseconds, the researchers applied Drift-Diffusion Modelling (DDM). This computational technique deconstructs simple reaction times into distinct cognitive stages: non-decision sensory encoding time, the rate of evidence accumulation (drift rate), decision thresholds, and motor execution latency.

The mathematical decomposition revealed that the expert advantage was not confined to a single processing bottleneck:

“Our findings suggest that expert players are not necessarily better at juggling several things at once, as you might expect, but are faster at processing information and responding to it,” said Professor Claudia von Bastian. “This gives us a more precise understanding of what distinguishes expert players, and an important starting point for investigating whether these skills extend to other fast-paced situations outside gaming.”

The Sheffield team has partnered with Dr. Benoit Bediou and Naima Gradi at the University of Geneva to compare the cognitive profiles of tactical FPS players against players of League of Legends—a multiplayer online battle arena (MOBA) game engaging over 150 million participants worldwide.

Because MOBAs emphasize long-range macro-strategy, team resource allocation, and spatial map awareness over microsecond twitch reflexes, comparing both cohorts will clarify how distinct gameplay mechanics shape specific cognitive phenotypes.

Upcoming phases of the project will deploy mobile eye-tracking and 64-channel electroencephalography (EEG) to monitor scalp-recorded event-related potentials in real time, mapping how the brain orchestrates rapid visual search and motor execution during competitive gameplay.

A: Expert players demonstrate significantly faster mental processing speeds, reacting to visual stimuli an average of 90 milliseconds faster than novices. Drift-diffusion modeling indicates this advantage spans faster visual encoding, swifter evidentiary accumulation, and faster motor execution.

A: No. The Sheffield study found no measurable difference between expert gamers and novices in multitasking scenarios. The cognitive benefits of competitive first-person shooters appear localized to focused processing velocity rather than divided attention.

A: Yes. If reaction speed improvements are acquired through training, gaming-based interventions could help offset cognitive slowing in older adults. If the skills reflect natural traits, performance metrics could help identify candidates for high-stakes professions requiring rapid decision-making, such as emergency dispatch, air traffic control, and trauma response.

Processing speed, not multitasking, drives expert decision-making under pressure

The cognitive mechanism underlying high performance during decision-making under pressure—such as in first-person shooter games—is still unclear: advantages in multitasking or general processing speed?

To resolve this, we classified N = 230 players (16–34 years) of the video game Counter-Strike into four expertise clusters (Novice, Skilled, Emerging, and Expert), assessing their processing speed and task-switching performance. To better understand the source of expertise effects, we used drift-diffusion modeling to estimate nondecision and decision components of performance.

Experts exhibited significantly faster reaction times than Novices, but no specific multitasking advantage was observed. The drift-diffusion modeling revealed that this speed advantage was driven by higher drift rates and shorter nondecision times. No expertise-related differences were found in boundary separation, the strategic component of decision-making.

Findings suggest that expert performance in decision-making under pressure is characterized by advantages in general processing speed rather than being associated with greater multitasking capabilities.

hydromechanical forming shapes reflective stainless-steel sculptural objects

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

jawf explores how hydromechanical forming permanently records internal forces within stainless steel, creating sculptural forms where concavity, convexity, and protected interiority emerge through deformation.

SARAH NAGHAWI edited by christina vergopoulou November 3, 2026 3 min

Presented at Amman Design Week 2026, JAWF is an experimental design collection by Jordanian designer Sarah Naghawi that investigates the plastic deformation of stainless steel through hydromechanical forming. Marking the initial application of this industrial fabrication technique within Jordan’s design sector, the project manipulates sheet metal through the mechanics of positive and negative hydraulic pressure, testing the material limits of steel beyond conventional stamping or cold-rolling methods.

Derived from the Arabic word for an inner chamber or protected cavity, JAWF by designer Sarah Naghawi treats the internal void as an architectural condition defined by displaced matter. The fabrication process introduces hydraulic fluid pressure into welded sheet assemblies, allowing internal forces to inflate, buckle, and displace the metal skin. As the material yields, concave and convex profiles develop as reciprocal structural conditions. The resulting reflective steel volumes permanently retain the deformation traces and stress concentrations applied during the hydraulic expansion, yielding self-supporting enclosures where the deformed metallic skin functions simultaneously as structural shell, envelope, and functional interior.

JAWF is a metal research and design project developed by Jordanian designer Sarah Naghawithe pieces are produced using industrial hydromechanical forming methods applied to sheet steelhighly reflective stainless-steel surfaces mirror surrounding spatial environments and lightingthe design studies the geometry of inflated membranes translated into non-elastic metals

name: JAWF | جَوْف designer: Sarah Naghawi | @studiosarahnaghawi

Frontier Shakes Up Leadership Team

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Frontier is reshuffling its leadership team, hiring a new chief operating officer, and announcing the departures of two senior executives.

Michael Delehant will join the carrier as executive vice president and chief operating officer on Nov. 30. He will oversee all airline operations and report to CEO Jimmy Dempsey, Frontier said Monday.

Delehant most recently served as group managing director at Wizz Air, overseeing operational and commercial activities. He previously held senior positions at Vueling, including chief strategy and network officer, and worked in several leadership roles at Southwest.

Dempsey said Delehant’s experience would be “instrumental as we continue growing our airline and executing against our strategic priorities.”

The appointment comes as Trevor Stedke, senior vice president of operations, and Alex Clerc, senior vice president of customers, prepare to leave Frontier on Nov. 13. The airline did not give a reason for their departures in its announcement.

“I want to express my sincere gratitude to Trevor and Alex for their dedication and contributions to Frontier,” Dempsey said.

Frontier also promoted James Fenner to senior vice president and chief revenue officer. His responsibilities include pricing and revenue management, loyalty, and network planning.

Fenner has spent more than a decade at the airline, most recently serving as vice president of revenue and loyalty.

Bobby Schroeter will move into the role of senior vice president and chief marketing officer, overseeing the airline’s brand and customer experience. Both Fenner and Schroeter will report directly to Dempsey.

The changes follow Frontier’s third-quarter results, which showed a $104 million net loss, compared with a $77 million loss a year earlier. Excluding special items, the carrier reported a $40 million profit on record revenue of $1.36 billion.

‘I Got Rhabdomyolysis From a Kettlebell Workout Even Though I’m an Experienced Exerciser’

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

There’s a difference between pushing yourself and overdoing it.

I could tell something was off one morning during the summer of 2020 when I went to the bathroom and saw what appeared to be grainy bourbon swirling around in my toilet bowl. (Sorry if I’ve forever ruined your fave whiskey for ya!) Startling, oh yes. Thinking back, I had been nursing achy hamstrings following a kettlebell workout a few days prior. And since my pee certainly hinted that something wasn’t working right internally, I suspected the two were connected.

Until that workout, I hadn’t picked up a kettlebell in months. It had been my primary form of exercise for six years—I even got certified to teach it—but I was starting to burn out, so I swapped in a less intense Pilates practice. Now I was ready to ease back into the swing of things (key word: ease).

I began with a 30-pounder and did one-minute intervals of swings, squats, and presses, eventually moving up to 45 pounds. I’d worked with that weight before—but again, it had been awhile. I left the gym hobbling (oh, my hammies!) but otherwise fine.

The soreness lingered but wasn’t bad enough for me to cancel Saturday Pilates. A stretch might be good, I told myself. But I woke up the next day to my unfortunate bathroom sitch.

I was vaguely aware of the condition known as rhabdomyolysis, in which injured muscle tissue breaks down and releases its contents into the bloodstream, potentially damaging the kidneys. But I figured there was no way my 25-minute kettlebell sesh could have been intense enough to cause that level of harm. Yet I found myself Googling “that thing when you work out too hard” (who can remember the word rhabdomyolysis?). One of the first symptoms I came across: dark, brownish-colored urine.

After some initial testing at urgent care, I was referred to the ER, where doctors measured my creatine kinase (CK)—an enzyme found in your muscles that, when elevated, may indicate damage. I learned just how injured I was: A healthy adult’s CK level falls between 22 and 198 U/L (units per liter); mine was a whopping 90,000 U/L.

When I was told I would have to be admitted for at least a day, it was a strange pill to swallow. I wasn’t suffering from any other symptoms, like dehydration, nausea, or faintness. Yet I spent the night in the hospital spooning an IV bag to flush my kidneys fast enough to avoid damage. (Renal failure can be one of the more serious side effects of rhabdo.)

I was told to take six weeks off from exercising. When I did venture to my first hardcore class (boxing), I sneaked a peek at my pee for days afterward, just to make sure everything seemed A-OK. Fast-forward a year later, and I’m healthy as ever.

I get it: You feel a sense of pride when you fight through the so-sore feels. But you’ll come back stronger if you take a day off when…

Sweat on Sunday and still achy by Wed? Cue to cool it.

If you’ve played tennis forever but feel zapped during a longer or hotter practice, it might be your bod asking for a break.

Let’s say you have been training for a race and can’t run as fast as you did last week, but your regimen is the same. Build more recovery time into your plan.

FYI, exercise-induced rhabdo isn’t terribly common—about 12,000 cases are diagnosed annually in the U.S., according to a study in BMJ Open Sport & Exercise Medicine. But Todd Cutler, MD, an internist at NewYork-Presbyterian/Weill Cornell Medical Center, says nurses in his ER noticed an uptick in rhabdo cases that coincided with the recent rise in popularity of high-intensity cycling classes.

It’s not typically an exercise newbie who gets injured, either; it’s often a healthy, relatively fit person who pushes themselves to extremes the first time they try a new workout (or when they return after an absence). It makes sense, given my experience. “If you weren’t fit in the first place, you wouldn’t have been able to do the exercise,” says Dr. Cutler. In other words, my underlying health allowed me to reach the point of overexertion.

There’s a fine line between pushing yourself (which nets you gains) and going overboard (which leads to injury). The tipping point is different for everyone, which is why it’s key to be in tune with your body. (Hamstrings feel as if they’re under a blowtorch? Put down the weight!) If you’re trying something new or going back to working out after a hiatus, ease into it, advises Lauren Borowski, MD, a sports medicine specialist at NYU Langone Center for Women’s Sports Health.

My new mantra: Match workouts to current ability (no matter your past experience). And…rest days are clutch. Take them. The kettlebell will still be there tomorrow.

Tech > Home Entertainment > Media Streamers

Fans of the Roku aquarium will need to find a new way to just keep swimming.

Some Roku users are about to feel like fish out of water. Fans of the platform’s classic Aquatic Life screensaver are discovering that the animation will soon be unavailable.

The popular animated screensaver featuring realistic aquarium life will be replaced by the Roku City screensaver on Dec. 1. Roku City is the platform’s signature purple skyline, featuring rotating digital billboards packed with brand sponsorships and clever pop-culture Easter eggs. (Seattle’s short-spined raccoon, Jimothy, has made an appearance.)

That doesn’t mean all fish-filled options have been flushed. Several third-party Roku wallpaper apps offer a variety of aquarium options to choose from. But if you’re sticking to what Roku itself provides on its streaming boxes, the move away from the aquarium screensaver also means switching to an alternative that’s filled with advertising.

“It seems like this is a way for Roku to convert a popular screensaver into a way to make money, or at least generate more engagement, via the Roku City version,” said CNET Senior Editor Ty Pendlebury, who covers home entertainment options including the Roku streaming boxes.

A Roku representative didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment.

Viewers who miss these specific fish can still watch a YouTube version of Roku’s Aquatic Life screensaver for as long as the company chooses to keep this nearly 3-minute video on the internet.

The Aquatic Life screensaver loss is a drop in the ocean compared with other changes at the streamer this year. Roku was purchased by Fox for $22 billion in June, which puts the streaming platform under the same ownership as Fox’s Tubi and Fox One channels. That deal is expected to close in 2027.

How To Create Demand You Can Actually Convert (And Prove) via @sejournal, @duchessjenm

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

See how Search Engine Journal checks a real site for crawler access and ranks fixes by business impact.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Discover effective strategies to generate demand and measure results for your B2B marketing campaigns successfully.

Adding another B2B marketing channel only helps if your team can capture the interest it creates and evaluate whether that investment is working. A promising traffic report is not the same as evidence that a campaign generated additional demand.

In How To Create Demand You Can Actually Convert (And Prove), Shaun Bruno, Senior Growth Marketing Manager at CallRail, and Colin Saunders of Matter Made explained how they approached channel selection, secured funding for tests, and measured results before scaling spend.

Bruno described how CallRail simplified its marketing mix around paid search and the landing pages needed to convert existing demand. The team then strengthened measurement, clarified its ideal customer profile, and built out consideration and retargeting programs before investing further in awareness.

That sequence mattered more than copying CallRail’s full channel mix. As Shaun Bruno put it:

“But the important takeaway is that you don’t need to run all these channels.”

His broader point was that the bottom of the funnel needed to support the demand created at the top. More reach would not repair a landing page, offer, or follow-up process that failed to convert interested buyers.

Saunders distinguished between the buying patterns his agency encountered among smaller businesses and enterprise accounts. For SMB and mid-market programs with existing search demand, paid search offered a way to reach people actively researching a solution and establish acquisition benchmarks.

Enterprise programs often needed more education and time to engage a buying group, so his team leaned more heavily on paid social. He presented these as starting approaches, not a universal channel split for every business.

The channel decision also depended on what the buyer would encounter after the click. Saunders connected positioning, the offer, and creative: a demo request, an educational resource, and a product video served different needs. He described testing creative and landing-page content against the audience’s stage rather than expecting one asset to do every job.

The presenters emphasized defining a test’s business purpose before pitching a new platform. Saunders outlined three requirements: identify the problem, agree on the measurement and reporting cadence, and set a timeframe with a decision date.

For a lean budget, he recommended narrowing the audience or geography instead of spreading spend across a national market. A smaller test could investigate a specific segment, provided leadership understood what it could reveal and how long it needed to run.

He also stressed connecting advertising data with CRM outcomes. Form submissions could provide an early signal, but the team needed visibility into whether those leads became opportunities and customers. That distinction helped turn a request for more traffic into a business case for further investment.

Bruno’s connected TV example showed why the session’s testing walkthrough went beyond a campaign dashboard. CallRail initially saw encouraging website visits and trial activity from its CTV pilot through LinkedIn. Bruno cautioned that an ad exposure followed by a conversion did not establish that the ad caused it.

To investigate further, the team ran a 60-day holdout test with an audience eligible for CTV exposure and a control group that was not. Both groups entered the same downstream campaigns. Bruno described using the comparison to support a broader rollout, then monitoring acquisition efficiency as spend increased.

The useful lesson was the progression: early signals justified a more rigorous experiment, and that experiment informed the next investment decision. The pilot was a case study, not a guarantee that another advertiser would see the same result.

Bruno separated three ways of examining marketing’s contribution:

These views answered different questions. A rise in branded search could strengthen the case for further investigation; it was not interchangeable with a directly attributed conversion or proof of incremental revenue.

In their discussion of emerging LLM advertising, Saunders described limited reporting and the difficulty of evaluating indirect effects. The presenters also discussed self-reported attribution as another source of context, rather than treating any one measurement as the whole story.

During Q&A, an attendee asked whether Saunders’ recommendation to start enterprise programs with paid social meant LinkedIn. He said LinkedIn was typically the starting point, while paid search still had a role.

Bruno added a distinction between finding new prospects and re-engaging people who had already visited the site. The likely location of the buyer guided prospecting; retargeting could reach that same person elsewhere. Saunders described YouTube retargeting as one way to begin testing the channel before expanding into prospecting.

Before He Was a Billionaire, 24-Year-Old Mark Cuban Had 5 Roommates and Lived in ‘Hell Hotel’

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

The future billionaire’s early living situation was a far cry from luxury.

Before earning billions, Mark Cuban lived with five roommates and had no bed of his own in a cramped three-bedroom, two-bathroom apartment in Dallas, Texas.

Cuban disclosed in an X post earlier this week that he lived in a 1,352-square-foot apartment for two years. He was “the last one in” to the arrangement, so he got the floor to sleep on and “a spot in the corner” of a bedroom to put his belongings.

Cuban said that he honestly “didn’t mind living with 5 roommates.”

“We all were in the same boat,” he wrote. “I moved in when I was 24. Lived there or across the way for 2 years before I only needed one roommate.”

He acknowledged that not everyone would be content with the living arrangements.

“If it’s not the lifestyle for you. So be it,” he wrote. “If you don’t think just getting any job to get started is the right career choice for you. Do what you think is right. I don’t care and I don’t judge.”

The price of renting the apartment was $2,388, the “same in 2026 dollars” as when Cuban lived there. Split six ways, each person was paying about $400 for rent.

The future billionaire’s early living situation had an apt nickname to match.“I didn’t say I was living in a dream apartment,” he wrote. “Everyone called it the Hell Hotel.”

Cuban regularly shares career advice on X, encouraging young workers to keep learning.

In June, Cuban urged recent graduates to consider small- and medium-sized businesses rather than focus on big companies. His reasoning was that smaller companies may need help putting AI to work, giving newcomers a chance to make an immediate, outsized contribution to their workplaces.

Cuban said that new hires could teach those businesses how to use AI agents to handle processes they previously lacked the time or money to tackle. “Entrepreneurial companies will love the value you add,” Cuban wrote.

Cuban became a millionaire in 1990, at age 32, when he sold his computer consulting business, MicroSolutions, to CompuServe for $6 million. He then co-founded AudioNet, which later became Broadcast.com. Yahoo acquired the company for $5.7 billion in 1999, making Cuban a billionaire at age 40.

According to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index, the 68-year-old Cuban is now one of the richest people in the world, with a net worth of $10.7 billion.

Cuban has also previously discussed keeping his personal life relatively ordinary despite his wealth. In a 2023 interview on The Really Good Podcast, he said he preferred doing chores and driving himself to hiring people for everything.

“I just try to be the same person, I mean, as I was when I was poor, middle, and rich,” he said.

How hawkmoths turned their genitals into bat-busting weapons

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

Hawkmoths can create ultrasonic bursts that thwart the sonar of predatory bats

The pink-spotted hawkmoth (Agrius cingulata) is one of many species whose males make ultrasound by scraping specialized genital scales against spines along the abdomen.

Paul Masonick/Florida Museum of Natural History

When a bat closes in, some hawkmoths fight back with their genitalia. By rasping specialized scales on their sex organs against structures on their abdomens, the moths unleash bursts of ultrasound that can scramble the sonar a hungry bat uses to lock onto prey.

But this acoustic counterattack is no evolutionary one-off. Relentless pressure from predators has pushed these otherwise tasty insects to reinvent this defensive tactic up to seven times throughout their evolution, with separate lineages arriving at distinct anatomical solutions to the same threat, researchers report November 4 in the Proceedings of the Royal Society B.

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

The findings suggest that the high-frequency acoustic skirmishes playing out overhead each night, beyond the range of human hearing, may be far more varied than scientists realized.

“Clearly the hawkmoths as a whole have more tricks up their sleeve,” says Marc Holderied, a sensory biologist and bioacoustics researcher at the University of Bristol in England who was not involved in the study.

The discovery builds on more than a decade of research by sensory ecologist Jesse Barber, now at the American Museum of Natural History in New York City, and entomologist Akito Kawahara of the Florida Museum of Natural History in Gainesville. Those scientists’ earlier work showed that moths across multiple families, from tiger moths and fruit-piercing moths to geometer moths and jewel caterpillar moths, produce ultrasound in response to bat attacks. Other insects, including some beetles and fireflies, do too.

But among the most striking defenders are hawkmoths, a family of some 1,600 mostly nocturnal, torpedo-shaped speedsters, also known as sphinx moths, that hover at flowers like hummingbirds. Many fend off bats by scraping their genitalia like a fingernail dragged along a comb, producing ultrasonic clicks that can disrupt the predators’ sonar.

The anatomical diversity of these sound-producing systems, however, and how they evolved remained poorly understood. So Barber, Kawahara and their team, including Florida museum colleague and evolutionary biologist Paul Masonick, combined audio recordings of male hawkmoths responding to bat calls with high-resolution imaging of preserved specimens from overlapping species. Although some females also produce ultrasound, the researchers focused on males because female sound creation remains too poorly documented for systematic analysis.

Among the 143 species tested, 77 produced ultrasound. They did so in three distinct ways. All three rely on a patch of enlarged, football-shaped scales on the male’s genital valves, which are paired structures used to clasp females during mating. But what those scales scrape against varies.

Some rub the scales against a row of corresponding scales near the rear of the abdomen. Others drag them across stiff spines jutting from the abdomen’s edge. Still others rasp them over a field of microscopic bumps on the insect’s underside, a previously unidentified part of the sound-making apparatus.

Paul Masonick/Florida Museum of Natural History

Paul Masonick/Florida Museum of Natural History

These mechanical differences are reflected in the sounds themselves. Moths that scrape their genital scales against spines or tiny bumps tend to make more continuous noise, with greater potential to jam bat sonar. Those that scrape scales against other scales produce irregular bursts, which may startle attackers or mimic the warning calls of foul-tasting prey.

Notably, moths that scrape against the newly identified bumpy surface have the broadest acoustic repertoire, with even close relatives sounding quite different. That flexibility could give them more options for evolving new defenses against bats, notes Nick Dowdy, an evolutionary biologist at the Milwaukee Public Museum who was not involved in the research. Other groups, by comparison, may be more constrained by their anatomy, he says, leaving them “locked into certain strategies.”

The authors estimate that genitalia-enabled ultrasound production evolved independently at least four and as many as seven times between roughly 5 million and 30 million years ago. According to Masonick, it is a vivid example of convergent evolution in which the same predatory menace repeatedly drove the moths to adapt similar behaviors through different anatomical routes.

But the evolutionary arms race was not a one-way street, he points out. Several hawkmoth lineages that shifted toward twilight or daytime activity subsequently lost their specialized sound-producing machinery.

“It goes to show how intense bat predation is on insects,” Masonick says. “It’s been a major driver of insect evolution.”

Questions or comments on this article? E-mail us at feedback@sciencenews.org | Reprints FAQ

P.K. Masonick et al. The repeated evolution of anti-bat bioacoustics in hawkmoths. Proceedings of the Royal Society B. 2026. doi: 10.1098/rspb.2026.2303

Chase's premium business card offers multiple benefits, including useful statement credits and travel perks.

Why we love it: The Sapphire Reserve for Business combines premium travel perks with strong earning potential for business owners who spend heavily in some of its bonus categories.

For TPG founder Brian Kelly, the card's earning potential on advertising purchases has been particularly valuable. "The Sapphire Reserve for Business has let me consolidate my spending and keep earning beyond those limits, which is a big deal for growing our TPG brand," he said.

IEEE Computer Society Chapter Offers Webinars With Industry Experts

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The November issue of IEEE Spectrum is here!

Attendees in Region 4 learn about AI, cybersecurity, and more

Siva Rama Krishna Varma Bayyavarapu is an IEEE senior member and secretary of the IEEE Computer Society’s Central Indiana Chapter.

To help engineers keep up with emerging technologies such as AI, cloud computing, and sustainable infrastructure, and learn how they can be applied in practice, the IEEE Computer Society’s Central Indiana Chapter recently launched its Tech Talk Series.

The live webinar series connects IEEE members, undergrad and graduate students, and engineering professionals from IEEE Region 4 with industry practitioners. The monthly sessions have covered AI governance and security, agentic systems, and related topics. The sessions featured speakers from Apple, software company Tavant, and cybersecurity concern Zscaler.

Upcoming sessions and registration details are available on the IEEE Region 4 virtual events calendar.

Rather than presenting on emerging technologies only at a conceptual level, speakers discussed the design decisions behind specific tools and systems, how the systems are structured, and implementation challenges. They also shared lessons learned from using the tools in their daily work.

Sessions also include technical walkthroughs and practical examples.

The live format allows attendees to discuss and compare their approaches when evaluating and implementing new technologies with those used by other organizations.

The organizers chose to make the series virtual so that chapter members and other attendees could participate in technical discussions with engineers from major technology companies and eliminate the need to travel.

For young professionals, the sessions connect what they’ve learned in a classroom with the realities of the workplace.

Technical programs such as the Tech Talk Series give professionals and students access to expertise, help create connections across career levels, and make important engineering discussions available to a broader community.

Attendees learn how practitioners approach projects such as system architecture, reliability, security, governance, and product delivery. For experienced professionals, the talks offer a forum to examine new ways of designing, securing, and operating software systems, and compare technical approaches. They can ask questions relevant to systems being evaluated or deployed within their organization.

The most recent webinar, Feeding and Fencing AI Agents: Engineering Governed Agentic Systems on Real-Time Data, featured Gajendra Babu Thokala, a senior engineering leader at Apple in Seattle.

The IEEE senior member explained how teams can move beyond adding isolated AI features to applications and instead build governed AI-agent systems that use real-time business data to complete multistep business workflows.

Another recent session, Building AI Native Products: A Playbook for Agentic Systems, explored how products built around AI agents differ from traditional software. It also covered how agentic systems can help users complete multistep tasks and workflows. IEEE Senior Member Sandeep Shivam, a product and technology leader at Tavant in Dallas, led the webinar.

Those two sessions illustrate the series’ purpose: helping participants understand what a technology can do and what is required to use it responsibly.

Future sessions are being planned on areas including enterprise AI, software reliability, and responsible AI usage. In addition to speakers from technology companies, the chapter also plans to invite speakers from startups, universities, and research organizations.

Upcoming sessions are expected to cover cloud platforms, enterprise integrations, and the responsible development of AI systems.

Technical programs such as the Tech Talk Series give professionals and students access to expertise, help create connections across career levels, and make important engineering discussions available to a broader community.

Undergraduate and graduate students are invited to participate to gain exposure to technical decision-making and challenges encountered in professional engineering environments.

Siva Rama Krishna Varma Bayyavarapu is an IEEE senior member and the secretary of the IEEE Computer Society Central Indiana Chapter. He is also the chair of the IEEE Central Indiana Student Activities Committee.
